_source line 5706 · 2026-05-22T14:24:02.973Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EYFAzmyptdmNHS5e1UX8Eg`</summary>

```text
1	\vspace{-2mm}
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	\vspace{-2mm}
5	
6	%% Mechanism — why DDR exists
7	\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of base-model autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.
8	Controlled decoding ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition---shape features remain near-stationary (mean $|d|{=}0.05$) while drifting features maintain large effects (mean $d{=}0.94$; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy})---confirming DDR is not a sampling artifact; temperature dose-response ($\tau{=}0.5{\to}0.9$) modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
9	ANOVA variance decomposition quantifies this asymmetry: $\eta^2_{\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (${\geq}50{\times}$; Appendix~\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).
10	
11	%% Implications — what DDR enables
12	\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for base-model corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access.
13	Even with limited per-instance recall, the classifier supports batch contamination estimation to ${\pm}3$--$4$\% accuracy at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}); depth-stratified curation has been proposed as a prophylactic against model collapse \cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though no downstream improvement was observed (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}).
14	
15	%% Scope \& Connections — where DDR works and fails
16	\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-supplement}); DDR complements \citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics (Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison}); all three analyses observe qualitative saturation within a similar depth range (${\sim}$3--5 iterations), consistent with the mixing property of finite-vocabulary autoregressive processes.
17	\looseness=-1 Instruction tuning compresses DDR asymmetrically (6/7 features show $\rho$ reversal; Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}); QLoRA recovers to F1\,=\,0.89--0.91, suggesting depth signal persists in higher-dimensional representations; whether this compression reflects RLHF mode-seeking behavior or SFT diversity effects remains open.
18	Three depth classes represent the current practical resolution limit (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}); cross-domain signal persists (F1\,=\,0.74--0.75; Appendix~\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\,pp at 5\%, F1\,=\,0.614 at 10\%; Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
19	
```

  "old_string": "%% Mechanism — why DDR exists\n\\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of base-model autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.\nControlled decoding ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition---shape features remain near-stationary (mean $|d|{=}0.05$) while drifting features maintain large effects (mean $d{=}0.94$; Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy})---confirming DDR is not a sampling artifact; temperature dose-response ($\\tau{=}0.5{\\to}0.9$) modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nANOVA variance decomposition quantifies this asymmetry: $\\eta^2_{\\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (${\\geq}50{\\times}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).\n\n%% Implications — what DDR enables\n\\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for base-model corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access.\nEven with limited per-instance recall, the classifier supports batch contamination estimation to ${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% accuracy at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}); depth-stratified curation has been proposed as a prophylactic against model collapse \\cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though no downstream improvement was observed (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\n\n%% Scope \\& Connections — where DDR works and fails\n\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-supplement}); DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}); all three analyses observe qualitative saturation within a similar depth range (${\\sim}$3--5 iterations), consistent with the mixing property of finite-vocabulary autoregressive processes.\n\\looseness=-1 Instruction tuning compresses DDR asymmetrically (6/7 features show $\\rho$ reversal; Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}); QLoRA recovers to F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91, suggesting depth signal persists in higher-dimensional representations; whether this compression reflects RLHF mode-seeking behavior or SFT diversity effects remains open.\nThree depth classes represent the current practical resolution limit (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}); cross-domain signal persists (F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp at 5\\%, F1\\,=\\,0.614 at 10\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).",
  "new_string": "%% Mechanism — why DDR exists\n\\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.\nControlled ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), temperature modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nANOVA quantifies the asymmetry: $\\eta^2_{\\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).\n\n%% Implications — what DDR enables\n\\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access; even with limited per-instance recall, batch contamination estimation reaches ${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\n\n%% Scope \\& Connections — where DDR works and fails\n\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location; DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics, with all three observing saturation at ${\\sim}$3--5 iterations (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR (6/7 features show $\\rho$ reversal), though QLoRA recovers F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}); cross-domain signal persists at F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp at 5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit})."

cross-domain signal persists (F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp at 5\\%, F1\\,=\\,0.614 at 10\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 4,
        "oldLines": 15,
        "newStart": 4,

        "lines": [
          " \\vspace{-2mm}",
          " ",
          " %% Mechanism — why DDR exists",
          "-\\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of base-model autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.",
          "-Controlled decoding ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition---shape features remain near-stationary (mean $|d|{=}0.05$) while drifting features maintain large effects (mean $d{=}0.94$; Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy})---confirming DDR is not a sampling artifact; temperature dose-response ($\\tau{=}0.5{\\to}0.9$) modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).",
          "-ANOVA variance decomposition quantifies this asymmetry: $\\eta^2_{\\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (${\\geq}50{\\times}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).",
          "+\\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.",
          "+Controlled ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), temperature modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).",
          "+ANOVA quantifies the asymmetry: $\\eta^2_{\\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).",
          " ",
          " %% Implications — what DDR enables",
          "-\\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for base-model corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access.",
          "-Even with limited per-instance recall, the classifier supports batch contamination estimation to ${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% accuracy at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}); depth-stratified curation has been proposed as a prophylactic against model collapse \\cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though no downstream improvement was observed (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).",
          "+\\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access; even with limited per-instance recall, batch contamination estimation reaches ${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).",
          " ",
          " %% Scope \\& Connections — where DDR works and fails",
          "-\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-supplement}); DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}); all three analyses observe qualitative saturation within a similar depth range (${\\sim}$3--5 iterations), consistent with the mixing property of finite-vocabulary autoregressive processes.",
          "-\\looseness=-1 Instruction tuning compresses DDR asymmetrically (6/7 features show $\\rho$ reversal; Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}); QLoRA recovers to F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91, suggesting depth signal persists in higher-dimensional representations; whether this compression reflects RLHF mode-seeking behavior or SFT diversity effects remains open.",
          "-Three depth classes represent the current practical resolution limit (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}); cross-domain signal persists (F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp at 5\\%, F1\\,=\\,0.614 at 10\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).",
          "+\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location; DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics, with all three observing saturation at ${\\sim}$3--5 iterations (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).",

1	\vspace{-1mm}
2	\section*{Limitations}
3	\label{sec:limitations}
4	\vspace{-1mm}
5	
6	\looseness=-1 All eight generators are base (pretrained) models with $\leq$8.2B parameters; scaling behavior beyond 8B is unverified.
7	Instruction-tuned variants show substantially degraded depth signal (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), and Gemma-2-2B exhibits near-zero gen-1 F1 (binary F1\,=\,0.440; gen-1 nearly undetectable) due to tokenizer and attention differences (Appendix~\ref{app:9gen-scale}), indicating that cross-generator generalization is limited to the eight tested base models from five families.
8	
9	The evaluation assumes a fixed decoding regime (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$) with English Wikipedia seeds and an English-centric reference model (Pythia-410M); cross-temperature transfer degrades at mismatched settings (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-temp}). All experiments use English text; cross-lingual generalization is untested.
10	
11	Depth resolution is bounded at three classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+); finer-grained 5-class classification yields F1\,$\approx$\,0.50 with deeper depths mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}).
12	
```

All ``entropy'' features are reference-model NLL values (cross-entropy with Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \S\ref{sec:features}.}
8	\label{tab:features}
9	\begin{tabular}{llp{3.8cm}}
10	\toprule
11	\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Measures} \\
12	\midrule
13	\texttt{entropy\_mean} & Ref.\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\
14	\texttt{entropy\_std}  & Ref.\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\
15	\texttt{entropy\_max}  & Ref.\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\
16	\texttt{entropy\_min}  & Ref.\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\
17	\texttt{tail\_decay}   & Ref.\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\
18	\midrule
19	\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\
20	\texttt{hapax\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	\subsection{Task Formulation}
26	\label{sec:task}
27	
28	Given a text $x$, we predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).
29	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.
30	Depths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
31	
32	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
33	\label{sec:generation}
34	
35	We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}, \textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \cite{llama3}, and \textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \cite{mistral7b}.
36	These generators span five distinct architectures with a 13$\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B), trained on heterogeneous corpora; Qwen contributes three models (0.6B--8B) covering the full scale range, and under LOFO the entire Qwen family is held out (F1\,=\,0.686), verifying family-level generalization.
37	
38	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
39	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
40	We use shared seeds across generators so that cross-generator comparisons control for input content and isolate the effect of the generation model from source text variation.
41	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
42	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
43	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
44	We generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\,$\approx$\,0.27; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.
45	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
46	After filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.
47	
48	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
49	\label{sec:features}
50	
51	\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.
52	
53	We deliberately use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$11.6\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition.
54	Leave-one-feature-out analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}) confirms that both reference-model and surface feature groups contribute independently.
55	
56	We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\ref{tab:features}), selected to provide principled coverage of the NLL distribution---location (mean), spread (std), extremes (max, min), and tail structure (decay)---complemented by two reference-free surface diversity measures.
57	We evaluated six additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\Delta$F1\,$\leq$\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\ref{app:feature-candidate}).
58	
59	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
60	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
61	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.
62	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000 (the Zipfian mid-tail, avoiding high-frequency head noise and sparse extreme-tail counts).
63	
64	\paragraph{Surface features.}
65	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\_ratio).
66	
67	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
68	These features exhibit a \emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).
69	TTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.
70	
71	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
72	\label{sec:evaluation}
73	
74	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
75	We evaluate generalization via \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.
76	LOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\,=\,0.686; \S\ref{sec:results-classification}).
77	Generator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).
78	
79	\paragraph{Classifier.}
80	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.
```

```text
1	% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	As LLM outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions becomes essential for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \emph{generation depth}: we introduce \emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.
4	We decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, NLL location features drift monotonically with depth ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.55$).
5	Prior work documents \emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape invariance provides a cross-generator reference that enables coarse ordinal depth classification (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) under leave-one-generator-out evaluation; an end-to-end classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}).
6	Instruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models.
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

       %% Scope & Connections — where DDR works and fails

        "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\\vspace{-1mm}\n\n\\looseness=-1 All eight generators are base (pretrained) models with $\\leq$8.2B parameters; scaling behavior beyond 8B is unverified.\nInstruction-tuned variants show substantially degraded depth signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), and Gemma-2-2B exhibits near-zero gen-1 F1 (binary F1\\,=\\,0.440; gen-1 nearly undetectable) due to tokenizer and attention differences (Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}), indicating that cross-generator generalization is limited to the eight tested base models from five families.\n\nThe evaluation assumes a fixed decoding regime (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$) with English Wikipedia seeds and an English-centric reference model (Pythia-410M); cross-temperature transfer degrades at mismatched settings (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-temp}). All experiments use English text; cross-lingual generalization is untested.\n\nDepth resolution is bounded at three classes (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+); finer-grained 5-class classification yields F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50 with deeper depths mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}).\n",

Bold line: pooled mean ($\\pm$1.96\\,SE shading) across all generators, colored by DDR zone (blue: drifting, wine: intermediate, amber: near-stationary). Thin traces: individual generators. Spearman $\\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trends}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\\,=\\,30 neighbors, min\\_dist\\,=\\,0.3).\nPanel~(a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.\nPanel~(b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.\n\n\\endgroup\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 356pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4)}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=356pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) Colored by 3-class label}\n\\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\\,=\\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) A gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013gxvmNtGrH536sNv36Q5j4`</summary>

```text
1	% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains
2	\begin{abstract}
3	As LLM outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions becomes essential for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \emph{generation depth}: we introduce \emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.
4	We decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, NLL location features drift monotonically with depth ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.55$).
5	Prior work documents \emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape invariance provides a cross-generator reference that enables coarse ordinal depth classification (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) under leave-one-generator-out evaluation; an end-to-end classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}).
6	Instruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models.
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

      "content": "% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains\n\\begin{abstract}\nAs LLM outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions becomes essential for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \\emph{generation depth}: we introduce \\emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.\nWe decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, NLL location features drift monotonically with depth ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.55$).\nPrior work documents \\emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \\emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape invariance provides a cross-generator reference that enables coarse ordinal depth classification (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+) without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) under leave-one-generator-out evaluation; an end-to-end classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
      "numLines": 8,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 8
    }

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{table}[t!]
5	\centering
6	\footnotesize
7	\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are computed as per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (see \S\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals that entropy mean and the two surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate. All ``entropy'' features are reference-model NLL values (cross-entropy with Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \S\ref{sec:features}.}
8	\label{tab:features}
9	\begin{tabular}{llp{3.8cm}}
10	\toprule
11	\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Measures} \\
12	\midrule
13	\texttt{entropy\_mean} & Ref.\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\
14	\texttt{entropy\_std}  & Ref.\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\
15	\texttt{entropy\_max}  & Ref.\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\
16	\texttt{entropy\_min}  & Ref.\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\
17	\texttt{tail\_decay}   & Ref.\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\
18	\midrule
19	\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\
20	\texttt{hapax\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	\subsection{Task Formulation}
26	\label{sec:task}
27	
28	Given a text $x$, we predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).
29	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.
30	Depths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
31	
32	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
33	\label{sec:generation}
34	
35	We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}, \textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \cite{llama3}, and \textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \cite{mistral7b}.
36	These generators span five distinct architectures with a 13$\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B), trained on heterogeneous corpora; Qwen contributes three models (0.6B--8B) covering the full scale range, and under LOFO the entire Qwen family is held out (F1\,=\,0.686), verifying family-level generalization.
37	
38	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
39	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
40	We use shared seeds across generators so that cross-generator comparisons control for input content and isolate the effect of the generation model from source text variation.
41	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
42	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
43	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
44	We generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\,$\approx$\,0.27; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.
45	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
46	After filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.
47	
48	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
49	\label{sec:features}
50	
51	\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.
52	
53	We deliberately use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$11.6\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition.
54	Leave-one-feature-out analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}) confirms that both reference-model and surface feature groups contribute independently.
55	
56	We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\ref{tab:features}), selected to provide principled coverage of the NLL distribution---location (mean), spread (std), extremes (max, min), and tail structure (decay)---complemented by two reference-free surface diversity measures.
57	We evaluated six additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\Delta$F1\,$\leq$\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\ref{app:feature-candidate}).
58	
59	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
60	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
61	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference validation (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.
62	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000 (the Zipfian mid-tail, avoiding high-frequency head noise and sparse extreme-tail counts).
63	
64	\paragraph{Surface features.}
65	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\_ratio).
66	
67	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
68	These features exhibit a \emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).
69	TTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.
70	
71	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
72	\label{sec:evaluation}
73	
74	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
75	We evaluate generalization via \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.
76	LOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\,=\,0.686; \S\ref{sec:results-classification}).
77	Generator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).
78	
79	\paragraph{Classifier.}
80	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.
81	Alternative classifiers (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}); discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
82	
83	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
84	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\% bootstrap CIs ($n{=}1{,}000$).
85	All $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.
86	
87	\paragraph{Baselines.}
88	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
89	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.
90	
91	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
92	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
93

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	An estimated 7--17\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.
5	Base models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.
6	We find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \emph{location} drifts with depth while \emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.
7	
8	Distributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \emph{that} and \emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.
9	We introduce \emph{depth profiling}, classifying text by ordinal generation depth (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy (reference-model NLL), lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains an order of magnitude more stable ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$ vs.\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.55$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).
10	We term this location/shape asymmetry the \emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}), a structural property of fixed-parameter autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.
11	The stationary shape component provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.
12	We focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}).
13	
14	As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
18	    \item \textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into a location component that shifts monotonically ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a shape component that remains near-stationary ($d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features). Where prior work documents \emph{what converges} \cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \emph{what is approximately preserved} and enables depth detection without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.
19	    \item \textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.
20	    \item \textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, and model scale maps the operational envelope of depth profiling, identifying key challenges in instruction-tuned models, cross-domain transfer, and perturbation robustness.
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
5	\label{sec:results-classification}
6	
7	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
8	This 3-class metric is inflated by shared gen-0 seeds across generators (per-class gen-0 F1\,$\approx$\,0.94; \S\ref{sec:generation}); we therefore report \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout, excluding gen-0 entirely.
9	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B; SD\,=\,0.028), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
10	CIs reflect bootstrap sampling variability; the cross-generator range (9.1\,pp) captures the distinct and larger uncertainty of generator selection.
11	
12	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
13	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B; generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,1{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}
14	Single-generator transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}) confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact (mean transfer F1\,=\,0.810; LOFO\,=\,0.686); the cross-model advantage is only 2\,pp.
15	A scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
16	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 under 6-generator LOGO ($+$11.6\,pp vs.\ XGBoost 0.789; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming richer token-level representations capture additional depth signal, albeit requiring per-generator fine-tuning; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition, not the classifier, as DDR's primary contribution.
17	The 11.6\,pp gap likely reflects richer input representations; the relative contributions of representation, optimization, and capacity remain undisentangled.
18	
19	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
20	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control verifies the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
21	
22	\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}
23	Seed-level demeaning (F1\,=\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) both verify that the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
24	
25	\begin{table}[t]
26	\centering
27	\footnotesize
28	\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. Cross-generator binary F1: SD\,=\,0.028, IQR\,=\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}).}
29	\label{tab:logo-results}
30	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

49	
50	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
51	\label{sec:results-differential}
52	
53	\begin{figure*}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
56	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}
57	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
58	\end{figure*}
59	
60	\paragraph{Formal definition.}
61	For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \in \{0,1,2{+}\}$, we define its \emph{depth sensitivity} as $\text{DDR}(f) = |\rho(\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.
62	A feature is classified as \emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; these thresholds are reference anchors, not definitive boundaries---the two-cluster structure is robust across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$, supported by unsupervised $k$-means (silhouette\,=\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$), exhaustive enumeration of all 35 splits (this partition ranks first), and independent replication in all eight generators (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
63	Under autoregressive generation, recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical---we hypothesize this reflects the mixing property of finite-vocabulary autoregressive processes~\citep{markovian_chains}---predicting that location statistics should drift while shape statistics remain invariant (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
64	Shape features (extreme ranges, tail decay) reflect architectural constraints rather than input location, explaining their stability under recursive compression.
65	
66	The classification results above establish \emph{that} depth is distinguishable; we now ask \emph{why} by examining which features respond to recursive generation.
67	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a consistent dichotomy across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean (reference-model NLL; \S\ref{sec:features}), TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.
68	Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative} illustrates the DDR with a concrete generation chain.
69	The primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering cleanly separates three drifting from four non-drifting features; higher $k$ degrades quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\,=\,0.532; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
70	A domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\% for drifting features).
71	Multiple effect-size measures corroborate this partition (Quantification below).
72	
73	\begin{figure}[t!]
74	\centering
75	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
76	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; near-stationary features (amber; $d \leq 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled; gen-0/gen-1 sensitive) and tail decay (TOST 4/8) are boundary features within this group. This location/shape asymmetry---the DDR---enables depth classification without per-generator calibration. Marker shapes encode feature identity for grayscale accessibility.}
77	\label{fig:feature-drift}
78	\end{figure}
79	
80	Pooled Spearman correlations with depth corroborate the binary split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
81	drifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.60$),
82	while non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}).
83	The six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\rho| \leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.
84	Within the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
85	
86	\paragraph{Quantification.}
87	Parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on the split.
88	Cohen's $d$ \cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\geq}7\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}), with the partition robust under heteroscedasticity (Glass's $\Delta$ gap ${\geq}9.5\times$; Appendix~\ref{app:heteroscedasticity}).
89	Four features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.
90	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay is \emph{weakly stationary}, passing TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
91	Entropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.
92	\paragraph{Validation.}
93	Per-generator split analysis provides the strongest validation: each of the eight hold-out generators independently recovers the identical drifting/non-drifting partition (per-generator silhouette range 0.505--0.760, invariant across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
94	TOST equivalence testing confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8 ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$), reflecting generator-specific tail structure variation rather than systematic drift: the effect sizes remain an order of magnitude below the drifting group ($d \geq 0.76$), and the partition assignment is unchanged.
95	Permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$); reclassifying either boundary feature preserves the core split.
96	
97	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
98	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified CV, complementing the LOGO protocol in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
99	The collinear drifting triplet captures ${\sim}$1 effective dimension (PCA PC1\,=\,92.3\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); group ablation confirms complementarity for depth profiling: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only binary F1\,=\,0.697 vs.\ all-7\,=\,0.700; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table})---shape stability contributes to depth \emph{grading}, not detection.
100	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains \cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
101	
102	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
103	To isolate reference-model bias, we evaluate on a 5-generator subset excluding all Pythia-family generators---a stricter protocol than 8-gen LOGO.
104	Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable performance (F1 spread\,=\,0.013; Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref} in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}), with drift direction consistently negative.
105	Same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.3\,pp, but the DDR partition is fully preserved regardless of reference choice.
106	
107	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
108	All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
109	The DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).
110	
111	
112	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
113	\label{sec:results-baselines}
114	
115	We next ask whether existing detection methods---designed for binary real-vs-synthetic classification---incidentally capture depth structure, repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
116	The strongest baseline is a 4-detector + surface feature ensemble (binary F1\,=\,0.708), which pools four detection scores with TTR and hapax ratio; among individual detectors, Avg-LogProb provides the most meaningful single-feature comparison.
117	
118	\begin{table}[t]
119	\centering
120	\footnotesize
121	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; pooled F1 is slightly higher than per-generator macro F1 in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} because larger test folds from high-performing generators contribute proportionally more). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}
122	\label{tab:baselines}
123	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
124	\toprule
125	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
126	\midrule
127	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
128	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
129	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
130	\midrule
131	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
132	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
133	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
134	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\
135	\bottomrule
136	\end{tabular}
137	\end{table}
138	
139	\begin{figure}[t]
140	\centering
141	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}
142	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth. Inference costs measured on a single NVIDIA RTX 5090.}
143	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
144	\end{figure}
145	
146	The 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble (binary F1: 0.705 vs.\ 0.708; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost, surpassing all individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\,pp.
147	DDR's contribution is the decomposition into location-sensitive and shape-stable components (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), with classification performance comparable to baseline ensembles rather than a performance advance.
148	Ordinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 under this setup (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), suggesting limited advantage from ranking-aware losses at 3-class resolution.
149	
```

**Pure text fixes still needed:**


- R48-N2 (critical): Looking at the DDR threshold definition in results.tex lines 60-62, it already frames these as reference anchors with robustness evidence across the epsilon range, supported by k-means silhouette scores and exhaustive enumeration ranking. The circularity concern seems already mitigated by the formal framing, but I should double-check if the reviewer's specific concern was about something else in the narrative.

Actually, the core issue is that the same dataset defines and validates the partition simultaneously. While the text mentions k-means and exhaustive enumeration as supporting evidence, all of this analysis uses the same data. I need to either acknowledge this limitation more explicitly or reframe the thresholds as descriptive findings rather than inferential claims.

- R48-N3 (critical): The Discussion currently mentions Geng's work briefly in relation to factuality degradation and convergence dynamics, but lacks explicit differentiation. There's a comparison table in Appendix z.7, but the main text needs a dedicated contrast paragraph that clearly delineates how DDR differs from Geng's approach.

- R48-DIV1 (warning):  The phrase "structural property" in the introduction (line 10) needs hedging. Changing it to something like "empirical regularity of unaligned autoregressive generation" would be more appropriate.

- R48-W3 (warning): The practical value of binary F1 needs stronger grounding. The Discussion mentions batch contamination estimation (±3-4% at n=1000), but this could be expanded with additional context.

- R48-W4 (warning): The tail_decay rank range (100-1000) has justification in Method.tex line 62 about avoiding high-frequency noise and sparse extreme-tail counts, but it could be stated more explicitly.

- R48-W6 (warning): Language limitation...

Now looking at the next set of issues. The English-only limitation mentioned in limitations.tex line 9 should be more prominent, and I need to add a note about paraphrasing limitations that's currently missing. I should also verify whether references to FineWeb and Welleck have been added, and consider whether Table 3 needs a brief discussion comparing concurrent depth methods beyond just the binary detection baselines.

Given the 3-day deadline, I need to prioritize which items can realistically be fixed and dispatch the Paper Agent accordingly.

Some issues need experimental work — the cross-model chain confound, comparing against Geng's concurrent method, and the Qwen overrepresentation can be addressed with text acknowledgment. The page limit should be fine after the compression and whitespace fixes I've already done. Let me compile a summary for the user and get the Paper Agent moving. R48-N3 (critical) — DDR vs Geng 区分**\n文件: `discussion.tex`, \"Scope & Connections\" 段落\n当前只提 \"DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics\"。\n在这句后面加 1-2 句明确区分：Geng et al. (markovian_chains) 分析 convergence dynamics 和 depth bound；我们的增量是 location/shape decomposition → 实现 cross-generator depth classification without per-generator calibration。附录已有详细对比表（Appendix z.7），主文需要 1 句 crisp summary。\n\n**3. R48-DIV1 (warning) — overclaim hedge**\n文件: `introduction.tex` 第10行, `discussion.tex` 第7行\n把 \"a structural property of fixed-parameter autoregressive generation\" 改为 \"an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation\"（或类似表述，关键是去掉 \"structural property\" 这个过强的 claim）。introduction.tex 和 discussion.tex 各出现一次。\n\n**4. R48-W3 (warning) — binary F1 practical value grounding**\n文件: `discussion.tex`, \"Implications\" 段落\n当前只说 \"batch contamination estimation reaches ±3-4% at n=1,000\"。\n补一句 grounding：虽然 per-instance recall 有限（gen-2+ recall ~27%），但 batch-level estimation 在 corpus auditing 场景下更实际——审计关心的是 contamination proportion 而非逐条判定。\n\n**5. R48-W6 (warning) — language limitation**\n文件: `limitations.tex`\n当前第9行 \"All experiments use English text; cross-lingual generalization is untested\" 太短。\n扩展为：English-only evaluation limits generalizability; DDR's reliance on reference-model NLL may behave differently for morphologically rich or low-resource languages where tokenization and vocabulary coverage differ substantially.\n\n**6. R48-W7 (warning) — paraphrasing limitation**\n文件: `limitations.tex`\n追加一句：perturbation robustness (Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}) tests only random word replacement; adversarial paraphrasing, which preserves semantics while altering surface form, remains untested and may degrade DDR signal more severely.\n\n**7. R48-W4 (warning) — tail_decay rank justification**\n文件: `method.tex`, 第62行区域\n当前有 \"ranks 100--1000 (the Zipfian mid-tail, avoiding high-frequency head noise and sparse extreme-tail counts)\"。\n补充：this range captures the power-law regime of the vocabulary distribution where depth-induced compression is most detectable; sensitivity analysis across rank windows confirms robustness (详细见 Appendix 如果有的话，没有就删这半句)。\n\n**8. R48-W1 (warning) — Qwen overrepresentation**\n文件: `method.tex`, §Generation Chain Construction（第35-36行区域）\n当前已有 \"Qwen contributes three models...verifying family-level generalization\"。\n在 LOFO 那句后面加半句 acknowledgment：Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8 generators) is a limitation; LOFO holding out all three Qwen models (F1=0.686) provides the strictest family-level test.\n\n**约束：**\n- 不要改 Table 数字，不要改实验结论\n- Limitations 保持简短（reviewer 说只需要一小段覆盖 3 个点）——现有 3 段已经够了，在现有段落里扩展即可，不要加新段落\n- 改完编译检查页数不超 8 页正文"

}
